names of each output, of length n_output\\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'metadata': {\\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        ...\\n    }\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### Key Format Requirements\\n\\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \\n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\\n\\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \\n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \\n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\\n\\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\\n\\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \\n\\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\\n\\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \\n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \\n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\\n\\n## Python environment\\n\\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\\n\\n## Decoder Reference\\n\\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\\n- Optional arguments:\\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\\n\\n### Purpose\\n\\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\\n\\n### Consistency requirements\\n\\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\\n- Loading of data\\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\\n\\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \\n\\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \\n\\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \\n\\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \\n\\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \\n\\n---\\n\\n## Conversion Workflow\\n\\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Each step has explicit completion criteria. Do not proceed to the next step until the current step is complete. Use the exact step names below when documenting progress in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n**STATUS TRACKING**: At the start of each step, update its status to `IN PROGRESS` in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. When complete, update to `COMPLETE`. This helps you and the user track progress.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 0: Setup and Initialization\\n\\n**Goal**: Set up the working environment and create the notes file.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. **FIRST**: Create `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` with the template structure shown at the end of this document\\n2. Verify you can run `python3` and import key packages (numpy, torch)\\n3. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\\n4. **REMINDER**: Do NOT look at any files outside this directory\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\\n- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\\n- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\\n\\n**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Look for documentation or README files in the `code` directory\\n- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\\n- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\\n- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \\n- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \\n- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 1\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\\n\\n**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\\n- Determine available variables and their meanings\\n- Check data dimensions and data types\\n- Look for documentation or README files\\n- Note the data organization and structure\\n- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 2\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\\n\\n**Goal**: Extract key information from the paper and methods.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\\n- Look for and note to file:\\n  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\\n  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\\n  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\\n  - Variables important to experiments\\n  - Temporal alignment\\n  - Temporal binning\\n- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 3\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 4: Check for Consistency\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Compare your understanding of:\\n  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\\n  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\\n  - Analysis described in the reference texts\\n- Note and investigate any discrepancies\\n- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\\n  - Experimental and behavioral variables\\n  - Data processing\\n  - Data curation\\n- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 4\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 5: Mapping Planning\\n\\n**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Map source data elements to target format components:\\n  - Which variables contain neural activity?\\n  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\\n  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\\n  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\\n  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \\n- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\\n  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\\n  - Temporal alignment of trials\\n  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\\n  - Brain regions recorded from\\n- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\\n- Check for variables indicating valid data periods \\u2014 exclude invalid data\\n- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\\n- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented all decisions under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \\\"Step 5\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 6: Script Development\\n\\n**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\\n- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\\n- Include options:\\n  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\\n  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\\n  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\\n- Write efficient code:\\n  - Vectorize loops\\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\\n\\n**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \\n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \\n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \\n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \\n3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\\n4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 7\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\\n\\n**Investigate any issues**:\\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\\n\\n**Done when**: \\n- Decoder training completes without errors\\n- Loss decreases over epochs\\n- Accuracies are above chance\\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 8\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\\n\\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\\n3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \\n5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \\n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \\n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\\n\\n**Done when**:\\n- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\\n- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 9\\\"\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\\n\\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \\n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \\n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \\n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \\n- For each major processing step \\u2014 (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction \\u2014 identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \\n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \\n- Fix any mismatches. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\\n\\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \\n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \\n- Fix any discovered issues. \\n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \\n\\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \\n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 10\\\".\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\\n\\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\\n\\n**\\u26a0\\ufe0f IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \\n2. **Wait for complete execution** \\u2014 this may take significant time for large datasets\\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \\n\\n**Done when**: \\n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 11\\\" with a table of accuracies\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\\n\\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\\n\\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \\n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \\n\\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \\n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \\n\\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \\n\\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\\n2. Check temporal alignment \\u2014 plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \\n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \\n\\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\\n\\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \\n\\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \\\"Step 12\\\".\\n\\n---\\n\\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\\n\\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\\n\\n**Actions**:\\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\\n  - Key decisions and rationale\\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\\n  - Validation results and tables\\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\\n  - Dataset description\\n  - How to load and use the converted data\\n  - Output format specification\\n  - Key statistics\\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\\n\\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\\n\\n---\\n\\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\\n\\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. position-binned trial matrices. |\\n+| `dff` | `code/src/reward_relative/preprocessing.py` | PROCESSING | Computes trial-restricted dF/F with neuropil subtraction, `maximin` baseline, smoothing, and optional OASIS deconvolution to `events`. |\\n+| `multi_anim_sess` | `code/src/reward_relative/utilities.py` | LOADING | Loads saved `sess` pickles across animals for an experiment day and attaches dF/F, events, place-cell outputs, reward labels, and trial subsets. |\\n+| `quick_load_multi_anim_sess` | `code/src/reward_relative/utilities.py` | LOADING | Loads already processed multi-animal pickle files from the shared preprocessed data location. |\\n+| `get_trial_types` | `code/src/reward_relative/behavior.py` | PROCESSING | Computes per-trial reward outcome and environment morph from aligned VR data between `trial_start_inds` and `teleport_inds`. |\\n+| `get_reward_zones` | `code/src/reward_relative/behavior.py` | PROCESSING | Maps scene names to reward-zone coordinates and labels (`A`, `B`, `C`) for each trial, including switch sessions. |\\n+| `define_trial_subsets` | `code/src/reward_relative/behavior.py` | PROCESSING | Splits trials into trial sets based on reward-zone switches or halves for single-zone sessions. |\\n+| `get_timeseries_data` | `code/src/reward_relative/glmUtils.py` | PROCESSING | Builds continuously sampled decoder-ready behavior and neural arrays from `sess.timeseries['events']`, masking low-speed and invalid samples. |\\n+| `CircularRegression` | `code/src/reward_relative/decode.py` | PROCESSING | Paper decoder model for circular variables; confirms the reference decoding was done on neural activity against position-like targets. |\\n \\n ### Notes\\n-[Your notes here]\\n+- The reference repo is for **2-photon calcium imaging**, not electrophysiology. There is no spike sorting or ephys quality filtering in this code path.\\n+- `sess` is the base aligned session object. The README explicitly states raw `sess` objects **do not contain dF/F**; dF/F is computed later.\\n+- `make_session_pkl.md` shows the first saved object is `sess`, created from raw suite2p + VR files after manual suite2p curation.\\n+- Cell curation is manual in the suite2p GUI; `iscell.npy` is the curated cell mask used when loading suite2p outputs.\\n+- `multi_anim_sess` is the main post-processing stage used by the paper:\\n+  - loads saved `sess` pickle for each animal/day,\\n+  - computes dF/F,\\n+  - optionally deconvolves dF/F into `events`,\\n+  - adds position-binned trial matrices,\\n+  - computes place-cell statistics and trial subset metadata.\\n+- For single-channel data, `multi_anim_sess(..., calc_spks=True)` calls `preprocessing.dff(...)` with:\\n+  - `neuropil_method='subtract'`\\n+  - `baseline_method='maximin'`\\n+  - `subtract_baseline=True`\\n+  - `neu_coef=0.7`\\n+  - `deconvolve=True`\\n+  - optional `keep_teleports` per animal\\n+- `dff` restricts valid fluorescence to trial spans using `trial_start_inds` and `teleport_inds`. Baseline is computed **within each trial** after smoothing, min-filtering, and max-filtering. The resulting dF/F is smoothed again, then OASIS is applied to get `events`.\\n+- `append_session_data` adds behavioral timeseries aligned to imaging frames. The pipeline is therefore based on **frame-aligned** neural and behavior streams.\\n+- `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data` is the clearest reference for paper-style decoder inputs:\\n+  - neural data are `sess.timeseries['events']`\\n+  - trial boundaries use `trial_start_inds` and `teleport_inds`\\n+  - reward-relative position is computed per sample from the reward-zone start for that trial\\n+  - low-speed samples are masked out with `use_speed_thr=2`\\n+  - lick sensor artifacts are suppressed when cumulative licks are implausibly high\\n+  - samples with NaNs in neural data or masked speed/lick channels are dropped\\n+- Reward-zone labels come from `behavior.get_reward_zones`:\\n+  - `A` corresponds to zone `X` = `[80, 130]`\\n+  - `B` corresponds to zone `Y` = `[200, 250]`\\n+  - `C` corresponds to zone `Z` = `[320, 370]`\\n+  - environment names remain `Env1`, `Env2`, `Env3`\\n+- The Fig. 3 decoder notebook uses:\\n+  - experiment `MetaLearn`\\n+  - experiment days `[3, 5, 7, 8, 10, 12, 14]`\\n+  - `ts_key='dff'` when loading saved `multiDayData`, but continuous neural decoder inputs are assembled from `sess.timeseries['events']` via `glmUtils.get_timeseries_data`\\n+  - occupancy matching by reward-relative position bin for some paper analyses\\n+- Conclusion for conversion:\\n+  - **Yes, dF/F must be computed** to match the reference processing.\\n+  - The decoder-relevant neural stream is most likely the **deconvolved `events`**, not raw fluorescence.\\n+  - Trial alignment should use `trial_start_inds` as the start and `teleport_inds` as the end of each trial.\\n*** End Patch\\n\",\n/logs/agent/trajectory.json:957:            \"content\": \"Chunk ID: 9c2b4a\\nWall time: 0.2129 seconds\\nProcess exited with code 0\\nOriginal token count: to rewards evolves with experience, we performed two-photon (2P) calcium imaging of CA1 neurons expressing GCaMP7f in head-fixed mice learning a virtual reality (VR) navigation task (Fig. 1a\\u2013c). VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal\\u2019s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day\\u20091 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450\\u2009cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50\\u2009cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day\\u20093, the reward zone was moved within a \\u2018switch\\u2019 session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day\\u20095, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day\\u20097. On day\\u20098, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On \\u2018stay\\u2019 days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 group of mice (n\\u2009=\\u20093) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray \\u2018teleport\\u2019 zone (~50\\u2009cm\\u2009+\\u2009temporal jitter up to 10\\u2009s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\\n9:Mice were pre-trained on a \\u2018running\\u2019 task on a 350\\u2009cm virtual linear track with random black and white checkerboard walls and a white floor to collect a cued reward. The reward location was marked by two large gray towers, initially positioned 50\\u2009cm down the track. The mouse was rewarded for licking within 25\\u2009cm of the towers, otherwise, an automatic reward was given at the towers. The towers then disappeared and quickly reappeared further down the track. Covering the distance to the current reward in under 20\\u2009s increased the inter-reward distance by 10\\u2009cm, but if the mouse took longer than 30\\u2009s, the distance decreased by 10\\u2009cm, with a maximum reward location of 300\\u2009cm. Upon consistent completion of 300\\u2009cm distance in under 20\\u2009s, the automatic reward was turned off, requiring the mice to lick to receive reward. At the end of the track, the mice passed through a black curtain and into a gray \\u2018teleport zone\\u2019 (50\\u2009cm long) that was equiluminant with the VR environment before re-entering the track from the beginning. Once mice were reliably licking and completing 200 laps of the pre-training track within a 30\\u201340\\u2009min period, they were advanced to the main task below and imaging (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d., 5.8\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20091.9\\u2009days of running pre-training across 14 mice).\\n11:## Hidden reward zone task\\n13:The main \\u2018switch\\u2019 task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocampal remapping, each with visually distinct features from the pre-training environment. Both environments consisted of a 450\\u2009cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a \\u2018hidden\\u2019, unmarked 50\\u2009cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80\\u2013130\\u2009cm; zone B, 200\\u2013250\\u2009cm; zone C, 320\\u2013370\\u2009cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a \\u2018teleport period\\u2019 that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (\\u2018jitter period\\u2019) between 1 and 5\\u2009s (5\\u201310\\u2009s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50\\u2009cm giving the appearance of a gray \\u2018tunnel,\\u2019 before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0\\u2009cm.\\n15:Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n\\u2009=\\u200911 mice). Most mice began the task in ENV 1 (n\\u2009=\\u20099 mice; two mice (m17 and m18) began in ENV 2). An initial reward zone (for example, A as in Fig. 1e) was acquired for days\\u20091\\u20132 of the task. On day\\u20093 (switch one), the zone was moved after 30 trials to one of the two other possible locations on the track (for example, B). On the first ten trials of any new condition, including the first day and each subsequent switch, the reward was automatically delivered at the end of the zone if the mouse had not yet licked within the zone, to signal reward availability. Otherwise, the reward was delivered at the first location within the zone where the mouse licked. After these ten trials, a reward was operantly delivered for licking within the zone, and we observed that the mice often started licking before ten trials elapsed (Extended Data Fig. 1a\\u2013c). The new reward zone was maintained on day\\u20094. On day\\u20095 (switch two), the zone was moved to the third possible reward zone (for example, C), maintained on day\\u20096 and moved back to the original location on day\\u20097 (for example, A). On day\\u20098, the reward zone switch coincided with a switch into the novel environment, where the sequence of zone switches was then reversed on the same day-to-day schedule for a total of 14\\u2009days (Fig. 1 and Extended Data Fig. 1). Each switch occurred after 30 trials.\\n17:An additional \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 cohort (n\\u2009=\\u20093 mice) experienced only ENV 1 and one fixed reward location throughout the 14\\u2009days (Extended Data Fig. 1d,f).\\n19:We targeted 80\\u2013100 trials per session with simultaneous 2P imaging (described below), with one imaging session per day (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d., 80.5\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20097.4 trials across 14 mice, all imaging days). The session was terminated early if the mouse ceased licking or running consistently and/or if the imaging time exceeded 50\\u2009min. Before the imaging session, mice were provided 30 \\u2018warm-up\\u2019 trials using the task and reward zone from the previous day to re-acclimate them to the VR setup (using the pre-training environment on day\\u20091 of imaging). Following each daily imaging session, mice were given another ~100 training trials without imaging on the last reward zone seen in the imaging session until they acquired their daily water allotment.\\n23:We used a resonant-galvo scanning 2P microscope from Neurolabware with Scanbox software (https://scanbox.org, v.4.7) operated through MATLAB (Mathworks) to image the calcium activity of CA1 neurons. Excitation was achieved using 920\\u2009nm light from a tunable output femtosecond pulsed laser (Coherent Chameleon Discovery). Laser power modulation was accomplished with a Pockels cell (Conoptics). The typical excitation power, measured at the front face of the objective (Leica \\u00d725, 1.0\\u2009NA, 2.6\\u2009mm working distance), ranged from 15\\u201368\\u2009mW for mice m2\\u2013m7, 50\\u2013100\\u2009mW for m10 and m11, and 13\\u201340\\u2009mW for m12\\u201319. The task was imaged starting from day\\u20091 for all mice except m11, for whom imaging started on day\\u20093 owing to lower viral expression. For most animals and sessions, to minimize photodamage and photobleaching, the Pockels cell was used to reduce laser power to minimal levels between trials (during the teleport period), except for mice m11\\u2013m14 on task days 1, 7, 8 and 14, and mice m15\\u2013m19 on day\\u20091 and all switch days, for which laser power was maintained and imaging continued throughout the teleport period. Photons were collected using Hamamatsu gated GAsP photomultiplier tubes (part H11706-401 for Neurolabware Microscopes). The imaging field of view (FOV) was collected at 1.0 magnification with unidirectional scanning at ~15.5\\u2009Hz, resulting in an approximately 0.7\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20090.7\\u2009mm FOV (512\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009796 pixels). An electrically tunable lens was used to simultaneously image deep and superficial CA1 in two mice (m17 and m18), in two planes separated by ~27\\u2009\\u00b5m, with frames bidirectionally imaged at ~31\\u2009Hz interleaved in the scan for a sampling rate of ~15.5\\u2009Hz per plane. In all mice, the same FOV was acquired each session by aligning to a reference image from previous days before the start of data acquisition, with the aid of the \\u2018searchref\\u2019 plugin in Scanbox. This allowed us to track single cells across days.\\n27:The Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155\\u20132172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\\n29:To compute the \\u0394F/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20\\u2009s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129\\u2009s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal\\u2019s running speed, excluding 0.42\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.85% of cells (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d. across mice and days).\\n33:To avoid assumptions about data distributions, we used nonparametric tests or permutation tests in most cases, except when a Shapiro\\u2013Wilk test for normality confirmed that parametric tests were reasonable. In the main text, percentages of cells classified by remapping type are reported as mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d. out of all place cells identified on the specified days. Cells were excluded from certain analyses only if they did not meet pre-established criteria as \\u2018place cells\\u2019 (defined in \\u2018Place cell identification\\u2019), to ensure that included cells provided reliable information about position in the task. Averaged data in figures are shown as mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.e.m. unless otherwise indicated. For the distributions of sequence positions over days in Fig. 4 and Extended Data Fig. 4, s.e.m. shading is omitted for clarity, but the variance for these distributions is accounted for in linear mixed-effects models to quantify these plots (see \\u2018Linear mixed-effects models\\u2019). For data displayed as violin plots, the shape is computed as a kernel density estimate to the bounds of the data, the solid center line marks the median and additional horizontal dashed lines mark the inner quartile.\\n37:The number of mice to include was determined by having coverage of every possible reward sequence permutation (six sequences) with at least one mouse in the switch task. In addition, our mouse sample sizes were similar to those reported in previous publications. Mice were randomly selected to experience the switch task (n\\u2009=\\u200911 mice) versus the \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 task in which the reward zone was not moved (n\\u2009=\\u20093).\\n41:AP and ML ROI coordinates were computed as the 2D center of mass of ROI pixels, relative to each FOV. In multi-plane imaged animals, ROIs were separated according to their deep or superficial imaging plane. To estimate deep-superficial (dorso-ventral (DV)) coordinates in single-plane animals, we acquired a z-stack scan after the day\\u200914 task session that extended \\u2212100\\u2009\\u00b5m below (\\u221260\\u2009\\u00b5m below in one mouse) to +100\\u2009\\u00b5m above the FOV in the DV axis, in 2\\u2009\\u00b5m steps with ~50 frames per step. Each step was independently motion-corrected as described above, and the mean of each step was used to compile the 3D z-stack array. We then took the AP projection of each ML slice of the z-stack (that is, looking at the side view of the z-stack). We minimum-filtered (4\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20090 pixel window, ML\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009DV) and smoothed (2\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20092 pixel s.d. Gaussian) each slice, then detected the most prominent dip in brightness in the DV axis to approximate the location of cell bodies, under the assumption that the GCaMP signal is typically absent in the nuclei and brighter in the surrounding neuropil. This yielded a per-pixel estimate of the depth of the pyramidal layer center from the dorsal surface of the z-stack, which we then smoothed (40\\u2009\\u00d7\\u200940 pixel s.d. Gaussian) to approximate the cell layer \\u2018curvature\\u2019 across the FOV. The curvature estimate was registered to the mean FOV of each imaging session. Given that the imaging plane transects this curvature, calculating the mean DV distance of each ROI (across pixels) from the curvature provides an estimate of where each ROI resides in the deep-to-superficial axis.\\n45:The capacitive lick sensor allowed us to detect single licks. A very small number of trials with erroneous lick detection from damage to the circuit were removed from subsequent licking analysis by setting these values to NaN (~0.65% of all imaged trials, n\\u2009=\\u200981 out of 12,376 trials removed across 11 switch mice). These trials were detected by >30% of the 0.0645\\u2009s imaging frame samples in the trial containing a cumulative lick count >2, as this would have produced a sustained lick rate \\u226520\\u2009Hz. Remaining lick counts were converted to a binary vector and spatially binned at the same resolution as neural activity (10\\u2009cm bins), then divided by the time occupancy in each bin to yield a lick rate. We quantified licking precision over blocks of ten trials using an anticipatory lick ratio, computed as:\\n49:where Lickin is the mean lick rate in a 50\\u2009cm \\u2018anticipatory\\u2019 zone before the start of the reward zone and Lickout is the mean rate outside of this zone and the reward zone. The reward zone itself is thus excluded to exclude consummatory licks (Extended Data Fig. 1e). A ratio of 1 indicates perfect licking only in the anticipatory zone, a ratio of \\u22121 indicates licking only outside of this zone and a ratio of 0 indicates chance licking everywhere, excluding the reward zone.\\n53:For all neural spatial activity analyses, we excluded activity when the animal was moving at <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121. To obtain an activity matrix of trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009position bins for each cell, we binned the 450\\u2009cm linear track into 45 bins of 10\\u2009cm each. We took the mean dF/F or deconvolved calcium activity on each trial within each position bin (note that taking the mean activity over time samples is equivalent to normalizing by the occupancy within a trial).\\n55:We defined place cells as cells with significant SI65 in either trial set 1 (pre-reward-switch) or trial set 2 (post-reward-switch) in a session. On days without a switch, we used the first and second halves of trials. For the SI, we used the deconvolved calcium activity as this reduces the asymmetry of the calcium signal. SI was calculated for calcium imaging as:\\n59:where pi is the occupancy probability in position bin i for the whole session, fi is the trial-averaged activity per position bin i and f is the mean activity over the whole session, computed as the sum of fi\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009pi over all N bins. To determine pi, we calculated the per-trial occupancy (number of imaging samples) in each bin divided by the total number of samples per trial, then summed the occupancy probabilities across trials and divided by the total per session. To determine the significance of the SI scores, we created a null distribution by circularly permuting the position data relative to the timeseries of each cell, by a random amount between ~1\\u2009s and the length of the trial, independently on each trial. SI was calculated from the trial-averaged activity of each shuffle, and this shuffle procedure was repeated 100 times per cell. A cell\\u2019s true SI was considered significant if it exceeded 95% of the SI scores from all shuffles within an animal, pooled across cells (more stringent than comparing to the shuffle of each individual cell). For plotting place cell firing over trials (for example, Fig. 2), deconvolved calcium activity was normalized to the mean of each cell within a session and smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian.\\n63:To identify spatial peak firing of place cells, we used the position bin of the maximum unsmoothed, spatially binned dF/F, as this signal is the closest to the raw data, averaged across trials within a set (pre-switch or post-switch). There was no restriction to field boundaries, thus allowing cells to have multiple fields with a single identified peak.\\n65:# Trial-by-trial similarity matrices\\n67:Correlation matrices were computed using the spatially binned deconvolved activity on each trial, smoothed with a 20\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian for single cells and a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian for population vectors. This resulted in a matrix, A, of j trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009m position bins per cell. For population vectors, single-cell matrices were horizontally concatenated such that A was j trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009m position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009n cells. Each trial was z-scored across the position axis. For single cells, the trial-by-trial correlation matrix C was computed as:\\n73:We defined the cell remapping types shown in Fig. 2 and Extended Data Fig. 2 as follows: TR, significant SI before and after the switch, with spatial peaks (see \\u2018Spatial peak firing identification\\u2019) \\u226450\\u2009cm from each other before versus after; disappearing, significant SI before but not after the switch, with a mean spatially binned dF/F after that is less than the 50th percentile of the per-trial mean dF/F before; appearing, significant SI after but not before, with a mean spatially binned dF/F after that is greater than 1\\u2009s.d. above the mean in trials before (appearing and disappearing cells that had sufficiently reliable activity despite their rate-remapping to be captured by the RR criteria below were removed from the appearing and disappearing groups); remap near reward, significant SI before and after, with spatial peaks \\u226450\\u2009cm from the starts of both reward zones; and remap far from reward, significant SI before and after, not TR, with peaks >50\\u2009cm from the start of at least one reward zone.\\n79:For stringent statistics on putative RR remapping (Fig. 2c\\u2013i and Extended Data Fig. 2g\\u2013n), we restricted the set of included place cells to require significant SI in the pre-switch and post-switch trial sets in each session. We converted the position timeseries of the animal to periodic coordinates, setting 0\\u2009cm on the track to \\u2212\\u03c0 and 450\\u2009cm to \\u03c0. Spatial peaks for each cell were re-computed using binned dF/F at a periodic bin size of 2\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u200945 (corresponding to ~10\\u2009cm). Putative TR cells were removed by excluding cells with spatial peaks within ~0.698 radians (50\\u2009cm) of each other before versus after the switch. The periodic coordinates were then circularly rotated to align the start of each reward zone at 0, to measure the signed circular distance of spatial peaks relative to the start of each reward zone. For scatter plots of spatial peaks in Fig. 2 and Extended Data Fig. 2, points are jittered by a random amount between \\u2212\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u2009100 and +\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u2009100 for visualization only. We measured the circularly wrapped difference between relative peaks after minus before the switch, creating a distribution that can be thought of as orthogonal to the unity line in Fig. 2d,f. We compared this distribution to a \\u2018random-remapping\\u2019 shuffle, generated by maintaining the pre-switch peak of each cell and circularly permuting its post-switch firing 1,000 times by a random offset of 0\\u201344 bins. To define a candidate range of RR remapping variability, we computed the circular difference between the maximum and minimum bin of the distribution of true differences between RR peaks that exceeded the upper 95% of the shuffle, divided by two and averaged across switches. In an initial cohort of n\\u2009=\\u20097 mice, this produced a mean range of \\u00b10.656 radians (~46.9\\u2009cm) around 0, which is captured by a 50\\u2009cm threshold given our 10\\u2009cm bin size. This threshold was subsequently used to identify candidate RR remapping cells (see below).\\n83:As we observed variable remapping dynamics over trials after a reward switch (Fig. 2a,b and Extended Data Fig. 2a), which could affect SI scores, we relaxed the SI criterion for RR cells such that they were required to have significant SI either before or after the switch but not necessarily both. We next implemented two criteria to identify a robust subpopulation of RR cells. First, we identified place cells with peak spatial firing relative to the reward zone start within ~0.698 radians (50\\u2009cm) of each other before versus after a reward switch (within the orange candidate zone highlighted in Fig. 2d,f), allowing for some variability in RR precision. These cells could thus include \\u2018remap near reward\\u2019 and \\u2018remap far from reward\\u2019 cells (see \\u2018Remapping category definitions\\u2019). Second, to reduce the influence of noise in spatial peak detection and account for the shape of the firing fields, we cross-correlated the cells\\u2019 trial-averaged, spatially binned dF/F aligned to the reward zone pre-switch versus post-switch. We predicted that RR cells should have a peak cross-correlation close to zero on this reward-centered axis. We calculated a shuffle for the cross-correlation by circularly permuting the activity in each post-switch trial by a random offset between 1 and 45 bins 500 times, requiring that the real cross-correlogram had a peak exceeding the upper 97.5% confidence interval of the shuffle and that the offset of this peak was within five bins (~50\\u2009cm) of zero. This approach provides a metric for how stable each cell\\u2019s firing is relative to reward, identifying a subpopulation of cells whose fields were maximally Pearson-correlated in periodic coordinates relative to reward, in contrast to the TR cells, which were maximally correlated relative to the original linear track coordinates (Extended Data Fig. 2o\\u2013q). Place cells that passed both criteria were defined as RR.\\n85:## Relationship between distance run in the teleport zone and trial-to-trial variability\\n87:We measured the distance run in the teleport zone at the end of each trial by integrating the animal\\u2019s speed from the first frame in the teleport period to the last frame before re-entry to the start of the track. We then computed the trial-wise spatial peak error for each RR cell as the difference between the cell\\u2019s spatial peak location on each trial and its trial-averaged spatial peak location within either the trials before or after the reward switch (circular error converted back to cm). We next performed a Spearman correlation between the distance run during the teleport and the error on the following trial (Extended Data Fig. 2e,f).\\n93:Using the spatially binned, deconvolved activity smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian, excluding activity from movement speeds <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121, candidate place fields were identified as regions of \\u226520\\u2009cm that exceeded 20% of the maximum of the trial-averaged activity19 (over the 30 pre-switch trials or the final 30 post-switch trials). To include a given field, the cell was required to be significantly active within the field boundaries in at least eight (>25%) of the 30 trials, where \\u2018significantly active\\u2019 was defined as the raw, un-binned deconvolved activity at those positions exceeding its mean +1\\u2009s.d. across the 30 trials. For analyses of place field properties before versus after a reward switch, cells were excluded that did not have significant SI or a significant field both before and after the switch. The fraction of cell remapping types meeting these criteria on specific days is reported in Extended Data Fig. 3c. For analyses of place field width and in-field activity rate, cells were further excluded if one of their fields overlapped the first or last spatial bin of the track, as this prevented accurate detection of field boundaries that extended into the teleport zone (which was not imaged for some animals; see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). Place field width was defined as the last minus the first position bin that crossed the initial 20% activity threshold.\\n100:To quantify the degree of backward and forward shifting of place fields following a reward zone switch, we first identified the formation lap (trial) of each field post-switch by adapting a previously established procedure31. Within the bounds of each trial-averaged field \\u00b110\\u2009cm after the switch, we identified trials in which the post-switch spatially binned activity exceeded its mean +1\\u2009s.d. We then found the first five-trial window with at least three trials above this threshold and took the first active trial in that window as the formation lap31. To compute the field\\u2019s center of mass on each lap with maximum spatial resolution, we used the raw deconvolved activity and position within the bounds of the trial-averaged field. We then subtracted the center of mass on the formation lap from the center of mass of the field\\u2019s mean activity in the last 30 trials of the session to quantify the degree of shift (negative values indicate a backwards shift).\\n104:We implemented a previously developed decoder that uses circular\\u2013linear regression38 to predict the animal\\u2019s position in circular, RR coordinates from the deconvolved calcium event timeseries (at speeds of >2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121) of RR, TR or non-RR remapping neurons. The model \\u2018decode score\\u2019 at each timepoint t is given by \\\\cos ({{y}_{t}}-\\\\hat{{y}}_{t}), where is the animal\\u2019s true RR position and \\\\hat{{y}}_{t} is the predicted RR position. A score of one indicates perfect prediction and zero indicates random prediction. We performed tenfold cross-validation in which we trained the decoder on 90% of the data and tested it on a held-out 10%, either training and testing within the trials before the switch or training on one set (before) and testing on the other set (after). Figure 3a shows decoder performance from a single cross-validation fold, considering all data from an example session. To quantify decoding accuracy compared to chance, we next downsampled the data to match occupancy at each RR position bin (bin size 2\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u200945, ~10\\u2009cm), either within trials before the switch or in the last 30 trials after the switch (thereby matching trial numbers before and after). We then repeated model training and testing with 100 shuffled datasets for each session and cell subpopulation. In each shuffle, the timeseries of each cell was independently circularly shifted by a uniform random amount up to the number of samples in the session minus one. In Fig. 3b, model performance is reported as a mean decode score over time samples and cross-validation folds compared to the mean of the shuffles for each session. To evaluate decoder performance across sessions, we z-scored the decoder score of each session to its shuffle and binned the z-score as a function of RR position, then averaged across animals (Fig. 3c).\\n108:For analysis of behavioral timescale sequential firing of neural subpopulations, we used the unsmoothed, spatially binned dF/F in circular track coordinates (not aligned to reward, although aligning to reward makes no difference for the circular sequence order). We sorted neurons by their peak firing positions from activity averaged over the odd trials before the switch. This sort was cross-validated by plotting the trial-averaged activity of the even trials before the switch (for example, left-hand columns of Fig. 4a, normalized to the mean activity of each cell within a session), then applying this sort to the trials after the switch. The sequence positions before versus after were then taken as the peak firing positions of the trial-averaged \\u2018even-before\\u2019 trials and the trial-averaged \\u2018after\\u2019 trials. Preservation of sequence order was quantified as the circular\\u2013circular correlation coefficient between the sequence positions before versus after. Although a P\\u2009value can be directly computed for this correlation coefficient, its significance was further validated by randomly permuting the cell identities of the neurons after the switch and re-computing the correlation coefficient 1,000 times to obtain a null distribution. The observed correlation coefficient, rho, was considered significant if it was outside 95% of the null distribution using a two-tailed test. The P value was calculated as:\\n114:For sequences followed across days, we again used the cross-validated sort from trials before the switch (or the first half of trials if reward did not move) on the reference day (the first day in each day pair) and applied this sort to trials after the switch (or second half) on both the reference day and all trials on the target day (the second day in the pair). To measure drift between days, we computed the across-day circular\\u2013circular correlation coefficient between the sequence positions on reference day \\u2018after\\u2019 trials versus target day \\u2018before\\u2019 trials. A minimum of five followed cells in the sequence was required to compute a correlation coefficient.\\n116:To quantify the density of sequences, we divided the number of cells with peaks in each 2\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u200945 position bin by the total number of place cells for that animal. These density curves were smoothed only for visualization with a one-bin s.d. Gaussian and are shown as the mean across animals per each switch day (Fig. 4 and Extended Data Figs. 4 and 5). We visualized the expected uniform distribution as the fraction of place cells in each animal\\u2019s sequence on a given switch day, divided by the number of position bins, averaged across animals.\\n120:To quantify neural and behavioral changes across task experience, we used linear mixed-effects models (mixedlm method of statsmodels.formula.api) to account for variance across animals. The fixed effect was usually either the switch index or day-pair index as a continuous variable unless otherwise noted. Random effects were mouse identity. In cases of only one fixed effect term, no corrections for multiple comparisons were made. Random intercepts for each mouse were allowed; we also confirmed that including random slopes did not affect the results. When the dependent variable was reported in fractions of cells, we applied a logit transform to the fractions when a large proportion of the values were near zero. In these cases, an expit transform was applied to the model prediction to plot it with the fractional data points. In most cases, however, we chose to use the original fractions in the model for interpretability, and we confirmed that performing a logit transform on the fractions did not qualitatively change the results and only modestly changed the model fit.\\n122:# Analysis of teleport periods\\n124:In the sessions for which we imaged the whole teleport period (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019), we re-identified place cells including activity during the \\u2018tunnel\\u2019 and part of the \\u2018jitter\\u2019 in the teleport period (see \\u2018Behavioral training and VR tasks\\u2019 for description), and re-categorized remapping types as either TR or RR from this set of place cells. Given that the length of each jitter was different, we only included spatial bins during the jitter that were occupied with fewer than ten trials missing in the session, using spatial bins of 10\\u2009cm starting at \\u221250 cm (the start of the tunnel) up to the maximum occupied bin (range, 530\\u2013580\\u2009cm from the start of the virtual track at 0\\u2009cm). We then re-performed the population sequence analysis (see \\u2018Sequence detection and quantification\\u2019), up to the maximum distance occupied across mice (580\\u2009cm); note that some bins during the teleport period will include data from fewer animals (Extended Data Fig. 5). To simulate the \\u2018ideal\\u2019 RR remapping destination if RR fields moved by the exact amount that the reward zone moved, we added the circular difference between reward zone starts to the pre-switch peak activity position of each RR cell.\\n126:# Analysis of rewarded versus omission trials\\n128:Analyses were restricted to trial sets (before or after the reward switch) that had at least three omission trials within the set.\\n132:We first fit five different time warp model types\\u2014shift, linear, piecewise one knot, piecewise two knots and piecewise three knots36\\u2014on the matrix of speed profiles within a trial set (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j linear position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20091, expanded in the third dimension for compatibility with the time warp algorithm). These models apply warping functions of increasing nonlinear complexity to stretch and compress the data on each trial for maximal alignment. We included both rewarded and omission trials in the fitting procedure to find the best alignment across them. Model fit was assessed using the mean squared error between the time-warped speed profile of each trial and the mean across time-warped trials (Extended Data Fig. 8a). The piecewise three knots model most often produced the best fit (lowest mean squared error) across sessions (Extended Data Fig. 8c). We therefore re-fit all sessions with piecewise three knots to ensure that model selection could not influence the results. We then applied the model transform to the deconvolved neural activity matrix for the same set of trials (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j linear position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009n neurons; excluding neural data at movement speeds of <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121). For neural analysis in Fig. 6, we then focused on trial sets before the switch in which the reward zone was at position \\u2018A\\u2019 or \\u2018B\\u2019, because the mean rewarded versus omission speed profiles were more similar on these trials than after the switch (Extended Data Fig. 8f) or when the reward was at \\u2018C\\u2019 (as there is less room at the end of the track for running speed to reach its maximum from position \\u2018C\\u2019 on omission trials; see Extended Data Figs. 1 and 8h for examples; see Extended Data Fig. 8 for analyses of trials after the switch and for all reward zones).\\n134:## Reward versus omission index\\n136:We compared neural activity on rewarded versus omission trials for RR cells with spatial peaks between the start of the reward zone and the end of the track (under the hypothesis that these cells could receive information about whether reward was received or not on that trial). Using the time warp model-transformed neural activity averaged across rewarded or omission trials within the trial set of interest, we calculated a reward versus omission index for each cell as:\\n140:where \\\\bar{{r}}_{j} and \\\\bar{{o}}_{j} are the mean activity in position bin j averaged across rewarded trials or omission trials, respectively. Reward versus omission indices of 1 indicate an exclusive firing preference for rewarded trials, \\u22121 indicates an exclusive preference for omission trials and 0 indicates equal firing between rewards and omissions. We confirmed that the reward versus omission index calculated from the model-transformed activity was highly correlated with the reward versus omission index calculated from the original activity (Extended Data Fig. 8e). Using a linear mixed-effects model (Extended Data Fig. 8j), we found that there was no significant change in the median reward versus omission index for each animal across days as a result of the model fit or as a result of the original mean squared error between rewarded and omission trials. We therefore combined neurons across days for presentation in Fig. 6e.\\n144:We implemented a Poisson GLM developed previously37 to predict the deconvolved calcium event time series of individual neurons from a set of task and movement variables. All behavioral and neural time series were sampled at ~15.5\\u2009Hz, the imaging frame rate.\\n148:Our design matrix included three task variables and three movement variables (Extended Data Fig. 9a). Task variables were linear track position (from 0 to 450\\u2009cm), RR position (from \\u2212\\u03c0 to \\u03c0, centered around the start of each reward zone at 0\\u2009radians) and \\u2018rewarded\\u2019, a binary that switches from 0 to 1 when the reward is delivered on each trial and stays at 1 until the end of the trial (stays at 0 on omission trials). Linear track position and RR position were separately expanded into 45 cosine basis functions, one for each 10\\u2009cm or ~0.14 radian bin. \\u2018Rewarded\\u2019 was multiplied by the linear position basis functions to yield the interaction between position and whether the animal had been rewarded on a given trial.\\n150:Movement variables were running speed and acceleration (smoothed with a five-sample s.d. Gaussian) and smoothed lick count (binary licks per frame smoothed with a two-sample s.d. Gaussian to approximate an instantaneous rate). Each movement variable was separately quantile-transformed to encode the distribution of movement dynamics similarly across animals. The quantiles were expanded with B-spline basis functions using the SplineTranformer method of Python\\u2019s sklearn package, with three polynomial degrees and five knots, resulting in seven bases (5\\u2009+\\u20093\\u2009\\u2212\\u20091) per movement variable. Spline choices were made for consistency with previous work37.\\n152:All predictors were concatenated into a design matrix with 156 total features: 45 position bases\\u2009+\\u200945 RR position bases\\u2009+\\u200945 rewarded-by-position bases\\u2009+\\u20097 speed bases\\u2009+\\u20097 acceleration bases\\u2009+\\u20097 licking bases. The features were independently z-scored across samples before model fitting on the \\u2018response matrix\\u2019 of deconvolved activity of all cells (samples\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009neurons).\\n156:Trial identities were used to group data for training and testing. Individual sessions (including trials both before and after the reward switch) were split into 85% training trials and 15% testing trials, using the GroupShuffleSplit method of Python\\u2019s sklearn package with a consistent random seed. Specifically, trials were allocated such that the training trials included 85% of rewarded trials before the switch, 85% of rewarded trials after the switch and 85% of all omission trials. The training trials were further divided for fivefold cross-validation during the fitting procedure. An optimal model was selected according to the deviance explained on the fivefold cross-validation data. We then tested this model on the held-out test data to assess model performance as the fraction deviance explained (FDE):\\n162:On average, the model\\u2019s FDE for all place cells was 0.10\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.19 (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d.) (Extended Data Fig. 9b,c), 0.32\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.13 for TR cells, 0.29\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.11 for RR cells (examples in Fig. 7a, b) and 0.29\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.11 for non-RR remapping cells. For analysis of the relative contribution of individual variables, we only included cells with FDE\\u2009>\\u20090.15 in accordance with previous procedures37.\\n174:We applied previously developed factorized k-means and distance score algorithms to identify remapping times at the trial-by-trial level. Here, we used the deconvolved activity matrices of each neuron (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j 10\\u2009cm linear position bins), smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian. We first scaled activity between 0 and 1 to be comparable across neurons, normalizing each neuron to itself by subtracting its minimum and dividing by the difference between maximum and minimum activity over the session. We then created a population vector for each subpopulation of interest (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009n neurons), used to create the population correlation matrices shown in Fig. 8 and Extended Data Fig. 10 (see \\u2018Trial-by-trial similarity matrices\\u2019). The shuffle was generated by random rotations of the population vector across trials38. Sessions in which the real model\\u2019s performance at k\\u2009=\\u20092 clusters exceeded that of the shuffle (two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test, P\\u2009<\\u20090.05) were accepted for further analysis (n\\u2009=\\u200950 out of 77 sessions for the RR population vector). \"Chunk ID: e35b60\\nWall time: 0.0000 seconds\\nProcess exited with code 0\\nOriginal token count: 2304\\nOutput:\\nmethods.txt:27:The Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155\\u20132172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\\nmethods.txt:29:To compute the \\u0394F/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20\\u2009s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129\\u2009s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal\\u2019s running speed, excluding 0.42\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.85% of cells (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d. across mice and days).\\nmethods.txt:87:We measured the distance run in the teleport zone at the end of each trial by integrating the animal\\u2019s speed from the first frame in the teleport period to the last frame before re-entry to the start of the track. We then computed the trial-wise spatial peak error for each RR cell as the difference between the cell\\u2019s spatial peak location on each trial and its trial-averaged spatial peak location within either the trials before or after the reward switch (circular error converted back to cm). We next performed a Spearman correlation between the distance run during the teleport and the error on the following trial (Extended Data Fig. 2e,f).\\ncode/notebooks/Ext3_FieldQuant.md:1031:  \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Find putative interneurons based on either correlation of dF/F\\n    with speed (under the hypothesis that fast-spiking interneurons\\n    in the pyramidal layer are often highly
